========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, what brings me in is... I keep planning my whole day around not having to drive over those high bridges. Like that big suspension one on the interstate? They built that back in, what, '89? My buddy Carl actually worked on the construction crew for it. He used to tell me these crazy stories about the scaffolding and how the wind would just howl up there. Carl's retired now, actually. Moved down to Florida, bought a pontoon boat... I don't know how he deals with a boat, honestly, the maintenance alone is a total nightmare. 

But, uh, anyway, yeah. The bridges. If I absolutely can't find a route around them, I just white-knuckle the steering wheel the whole way across. Just gripping it for dear life. But mostly I take the long way around to avoid them, which makes me late for work sometimes. And working in HR, I really shouldn't be late. We had this one guy last week showing up at 10 AM every single day, just strolling in like he owned the place, and I had to be the one to sit him down and talk about company time... but yeah, the driving thing is just getting to be a real hassle.

No, it's really just the bridges. Tall buildings don't bother me at all. Actually, our old HR office used to be on the 14th floor of this big high-rise downtown before we moved out to the business park. The elevators in that place, though... man, they were always breaking down. I swear, at least once a month somebody would get stuck in one. We had this maintenance guy, Hector—great guy, knew everything about HVAC—but he was always complaining about the elevator parts being on backorder. Supply chain issues, you know? 

It's like you can't get anything on time anymore. I ordered a new filter for my home furnace like three weeks ago and it's still just sitting in some warehouse in Ohio, according to the tracking number. And you really need to change those every few months or your heating bill goes right through the roof. 

But, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, heights. Yeah, no, mountains and buildings are totally fine. Just those high bridges for some reason.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I don't think they're going to collapse. I mean, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every single day without a problem. My brother-in-law commutes over one every morning in his Chevy Silverado. Actually, he just got new tires on that thing, those big all-terrain ones. He got a flat last month on his way to work and had to wait three hours for a tow truck. Three hours! I told him he should have just put the spare on himself, but he said the jack was rusted out. You really gotta check your tools before winter hits, you know? The salt on the roads around here just eats right through the metal. I usually take my car to the wash at least twice a month during the winter just to spray the undercarriage. They have this new automated one down by the grocery store that does a really good job with the wax, too, and it's only like eight bucks if you buy the monthly pass. 

But, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the bridge falling down. Yeah, I'm not worried about the engineering or anything like that. It's just... when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. Like I'm just going to veer off the edge or something.

Well, it's funny you ask that, because I haven't actually been a passenger over one in a long time. My wife usually makes me drive everywhere. She hates driving, especially at night. Her night vision isn't what it used to be. We actually went to the eye doctor last week, Dr. Henderson over in the strip mall next to the Target? Nice guy, but his waiting room is freezing. I don't know why doctors' offices always keep the AC cranked up so high. You sit there for twenty minutes and you're shivering by the time they call your name. Anyway, he told her she needs bifocals now, which she wasn't too happy about. She thinks they make her look old, but I told her, hey, they have those progressive lenses now where you can't even see the line. My buddy Carl has them and he swears by them. 

But, uh, anyway... what was the question? Oh, being a passenger. Yeah, I don't know, honestly. I'm almost always the one behind the wheel, so I couldn't really tell you if it's just as bad when I'm riding shotgun. I just know when I'm driving, it's... it's a whole ordeal.

Just those high bridges for some reason.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that the bridge will collapse while you are driving on it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think they're going to collapse. I mean, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every single day without a problem. My brother-in-law commutes over one every morning in his Chevy Silverado. Actually, he just got new tires on that thing, those big all-terrain ones. He got a flat last month on his way to work and had to wait three hours for a tow truck. Three hours! I told him he should have just put the spare on himself, but he said the jack was rusted out. You really gotta check your tools before winter hits, you know? The salt on the roads around here just eats right through the metal. I usually take my car to the wash at least twice a month during the winter just to spray the undercarriage. They have this new automated one down by the grocery store that does a really good job with the wax, too, and it's only like eight bucks if you buy the monthly pass. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the bridge falling down. Yeah, I'm not worried about the engineering or anything like that. It's just... when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. Like I'm just going to veer off the edge or something.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety occur when you are a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is it only when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I haven't actually been a passenger over one in a long time. My wife usually makes me drive everywhere. She hates driving, especially at night. Her night vision isn't what it used to be. We actually went to the eye doctor last week, Dr. Henderson over in the strip mall next to the Target? Nice guy, but his waiting room is freezing. I don't know why doctors' offices always keep the AC cranked up so high. You sit there for twenty minutes and you're shivering by the time they call your name. Anyway, he told her she needs bifocals now, which she wasn't too happy about. She thinks they make her look old, but I told her, hey, they have those progressive lenses now where you can't even see the line. My buddy Carl has them and he swears by them. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... what was the question? Oh, being a passenger. Yeah, I don't know, honestly. I'm almost always the one behind the wheel, so I couldn't really tell you if it's just as bad when I'm riding shotgun. I just know when I'm driving, it's... it's a whole ordeal.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf you were a passenger in a car going over a high bridge, would you feel anxious?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / human_resources_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\nmanifestation: \"As soon as I see the bridge coming up, my chest gets tight, my hands sweat on the wheel, and I start staring straight ahead like I just have to make it to the other side.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess I'd still be pretty worked up even if I wasn't the one driving. I mean, just the sight of those bridges gets to me. As soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating. Even if I was in the passenger seat, I think I'd still be gripping the door handle or something, just staring straight ahead, feeling like we just have to make it to the other side, you know? 

It's funny, speaking of the passenger seat, my wife has this habit of leaving her empty coffee cups in the door pocket on her side. I swear, every time I go to vacuum out the car—which I try to do every other weekend, especially with the dog tracking in dirt—I find like three of those paper cups from the Dunkin' down the street. I keep telling her, "Honey, just throw them out when we get gas," but she always forgets. We've got this great little hand vac, though, a Dirt Devil. Got it on sale at Home Depot last Black Friday. Best thirty bucks I ever spent. It gets right into the crevices of the seats. 

But, uh, wait... oh, right, being a passenger on the bridge. Yeah, I'm pretty sure my chest would still get tight and I'd be sweating just as bad, even if I wasn't the one steering. Just seeing the bridge is enough to set it off.

Oh, right, the bridge falling down. Yeah, I'm not worried about the engineering or anything like that. It's just... when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. Like I'm just going to veer off the edge or something.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety occur when you are a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is it only when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I haven't actually been a passenger over one in a long time. My wife usually makes me drive everywhere. She hates driving, especially at night. Her night vision isn't what it used to be. We actually went to the eye doctor last week, Dr. Henderson over in the strip mall next to the Target? Nice guy, but his waiting room is freezing. I don't know why doctors' offices always keep the AC cranked up so high. You sit there for twenty minutes and you're shivering by the time they call your name. Anyway, he told her she needs bifocals now, which she wasn't too happy about. She thinks they make her look old, but I told her, hey, they have those progressive lenses now where you can't even see the line. My buddy Carl has them and he swears by them. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... what was the question? Oh, being a passenger. Yeah, I don't know, honestly. I'm almost always the one behind the wheel, so I couldn't really tell you if it's just as bad when I'm riding shotgun. I just know when I'm driving, it's... it's a whole ordeal.\n\nDoctor: If you were a passenger in a car going over a high bridge, would you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'd still be pretty worked up even if I wasn't the one driving. I mean, just the sight of those bridges gets to me. As soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating. Even if I was in the passenger seat, I think I'd still be gripping the door handle or something, just staring straight ahead, feeling like we just have to make it to the other side, you know? \n\nIt's funny, speaking of the passenger seat, my wife has this habit of leaving her empty coffee cups in the door pocket on her side. I swear, every time I go to vacuum out the car—which I try to do every other weekend, especially with the dog tracking in dirt—I find like three of those paper cups from the Dunkin' down the street. I keep telling her, \"Honey, just throw them out when we get gas,\" but she always forgets. We've got this great little hand vac, though, a Dirt Devil. Got it on sale at Home Depot last Black Friday. Best thirty bucks I ever spent. It gets right into the crevices of the seats. \n\nBut, uh, wait... oh, right, being a passenger on the bridge. Yeah, I'm pretty sure my chest would still get tight and I'd be sweating just as bad, even if I wasn't the one steering. Just seeing the bridge is enough to set it off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas avoiding bridges or being late to work due to taking the long way around them affected your relationships with your coworkers or your overall job performance?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / human_resources_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I still get to work and see people, but I turn down some fishing outings and leave extra early because I take longer routes around certain bridges. It makes me feel less independent than I used to be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, it hasn't really messed up my job or anything. I still get to work every day and do my job in HR, and I see people just fine. I just have to leave extra early to take the long way around the bridges. It's not a huge deal, but I do have to wake up at like 5:30 AM now. Which is fine, I guess, because I like to catch the morning news anyway. You know, they have that new anchor on Channel 4, the guy with the really great hair? He used to do the weather in Chicago, I think. My wife thinks he's too loud, but I like him. He actually did a whole segment on how to properly winterize your lawn sprinkler system last week. I meant to write down the steps, because last year one of my pipes burst and it was a nightmare. Water everywhere, right in the front yard. Had to call a plumber on a Sunday, which you know costs an arm and a leg. He charged me double just for showing up. 

But, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, work and stuff. Yeah, my job's fine. It's more just... I don't know, it makes me feel less independent than I used to be. Like, I've had to turn down a few fishing outings with the guys because the lake is over the big suspension bridge. I just told them I was busy, but really I just didn't want to deal with the drive. It's a shame, too, because they caught a ton of bass last time. My buddy Steve has this new fiberglass boat, a real beauty. He got it at a boat show in February. I think he paid too much for it, honestly, but he loves it. Anyway, yeah, I still get by, it's just... annoying having to plan around it.

No, not really. I mean, I don't think it's about being in control, because like I said, even if I'm not driving, just seeing the bridge gets me all worked up. It's really just the bridges. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Walk or bike? Oh, no, I haven't tried that. I haven't ridden a bike in... gosh, it's gotta be fifteen years. I used to have this great mountain bike, a Schwinn, back when we lived in our old neighborhood. It had twenty-one gears and these really thick tires. I used to ride it down to the corner store on Saturday mornings to get the paper and a couple of scratch-offs. Never won more than five bucks, though. My neighbor, Tom, he actually won like fifty grand on a scratch-off once. Can you believe that? He used it to put a sunroom on the back of his house. It was really nice, all glass, but it got so hot in the summer you couldn't even sit out there. He had to buy this massive ceiling fan just to keep the air moving. I helped him install it, actually. We almost dropped the motor right on his glass coffee table. 

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, walking or biking over a bridge. Yeah, no, I haven't done that. I can't even imagine it. If just driving over one or seeing it from the passenger seat gets me sweating and my chest all tight, I think walking across one would probably be a hundred times worse. Just being out there in the open air, looking down... no thanks. I wouldn't even try it.

I don't know how he deals with a boat, honestly, the maintenance alone is a total nightmare. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, yeah. The bridges. If I absolutely can't find a route around them, I just white-knuckle the steering wheel the whole way across. Just gripping it for dear life. But mostly I take the long way around to avoid them, which makes me late for work sometimes. And working in HR, I really shouldn't be late. We had this one guy last week showing up at 10 AM every single day, just strolling in like he owned the place, and I had to be the one to sit him down and talk about company time... but yeah, the driving thing is just getting to be a real hassle.\n\nDoctor: Do you also avoid other high places, like tall buildings or mountainous areas, or is it mainly just the bridges that bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the bridges. Tall buildings don't bother me at all. Actually, our old HR office used to be on the 14th floor of this big high-rise downtown before we moved out to the business park. The elevators in that place, though... man, they were always breaking down. I swear, at least once a month somebody would get stuck in one. We had this maintenance guy, Hector—great guy, knew everything about HVAC—but he was always complaining about the elevator parts being on backorder. Supply chain issues, you know? \n\nIt's like you can't get anything on time anymore. I ordered a new filter for my home furnace like three weeks ago and it's still just sitting in some warehouse in Ohio, according to the tracking number. And you really need to change those every few months or your heating bill goes right through the roof. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, heights. Yeah, no, mountains and buildings are totally fine. Just those high bridges for some reason.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that the bridge will collapse while you are driving on it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think they're going to collapse. I mean, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every single day without a problem. My brother-in-law commutes over one every morning in his Chevy Silverado. Actually, he just got new tires on that thing, those big all-terrain ones. He got a flat last month on his way to work and had to wait three hours for a tow truck. Three hours! I told him he should have just put the spare on himself, but he said the jack was rusted out. You really gotta check your tools before winter hits, you know? The salt on the roads around here just eats right through the metal. I usually take my car to the wash at least twice a month during the winter just to spray the undercarriage. They have this new automated one down by the grocery store that does a really good job with the wax, too, and it's only like eight bucks if you buy the monthly pass. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the bridge falling down. Yeah, I'm not worried about the engineering or anything like that. It's just... when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. Like I'm just going to veer off the edge or something.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety occur when you are a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is it only when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I haven't actually been a passenger over one in a long time. My wife usually makes me drive everywhere. She hates driving, especially at night. Her night vision isn't what it used to be. We actually went to the eye doctor last week, Dr. Henderson over in the strip mall next to the Target? Nice guy, but his waiting room is freezing. I don't know why doctors' offices always keep the AC cranked up so high. You sit there for twenty minutes and you're shivering by the time they call your name. Anyway, he told her she needs bifocals now, which she wasn't too happy about. She thinks they make her look old, but I told her, hey, they have those progressive lenses now where you can't even see the line. My buddy Carl has them and he swears by them. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... what was the question? Oh, being a passenger. Yeah, I don't know, honestly. I'm almost always the one behind the wheel, so I couldn't really tell you if it's just as bad when I'm riding shotgun. I just know when I'm driving, it's... it's a whole ordeal.\n\nDoctor: If you were a passenger in a car going over a high bridge, would you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'd still be pretty worked up even if I wasn't the one driving. I mean, just the sight of those bridges gets to me. As soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating. Even if I was in the passenger seat, I think I'd still be gripping the door handle or something, just staring straight ahead, feeling like we just have to make it to the other side, you know? \n\nIt's funny, speaking of the passenger seat, my wife has this habit of leaving her empty coffee cups in the door pocket on her side. I swear, every time I go to vacuum out the car—which I try to do every other weekend, especially with the dog tracking in dirt—I find like three of those paper cups from the Dunkin' down the street. I keep telling her, \"Honey, just throw them out when we get gas,\" but she always forgets. We've got this great little hand vac, though, a Dirt Devil. Got it on sale at Home Depot last Black Friday. Best thirty bucks I ever spent. It gets right into the crevices of the seats. \n\nBut, uh, wait... oh, right, being a passenger on the bridge. Yeah, I'm pretty sure my chest would still get tight and I'd be sweating just as bad, even if I wasn't the one steering. Just seeing the bridge is enough to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding bridges or being late to work due to taking the long way around them affected your relationships with your coworkers or your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't really messed up my job or anything. I still get to work every day and do my job in HR, and I see people just fine. I just have to leave extra early to take the long way around the bridges. It's not a huge deal, but I do have to wake up at like 5:30 AM now. Which is fine, I guess, because I like to catch the morning news anyway. You know, they have that new anchor on Channel 4, the guy with the really great hair? He used to do the weather in Chicago, I think. My wife thinks he's too loud, but I like him. He actually did a whole segment on how to properly winterize your lawn sprinkler system last week. I meant to write down the steps, because last year one of my pipes burst and it was a nightmare. Water everywhere, right in the front yard. Had to call a plumber on a Sunday, which you know costs an arm and a leg. He charged me double just for showing up. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, work and stuff. Yeah, my job's fine. It's more just... I don't know, it makes me feel less independent than I used to be. Like, I've had to turn down a few fishing outings with the guys because the lake is over the big suspension bridge. I just told them I was busy, but really I just didn't want to deal with the drive. It's a shame, too, because they caught a ton of bass last time. My buddy Steve has this new fiberglass boat, a real beauty. He got it at a boat show in February. I think he paid too much for it, honestly, but he loves it. Anyway, yeah, I still get by, it's just... annoying having to plan around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety in other situations where you feel like you're not in control?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think it's about being in control, because like I said, even if I'm not driving, just seeing the bridge gets me all worked up. It's really just the bridges. I fly on planes just fine, you know? Actually, my wife and I flew down to Florida last winter to visit her sister in Tampa. We flew Southwest. You know, they let you check two bags for free, which is great because my wife always overpacks. She brought like four pairs of shoes for a four-day trip. We went to this amazing seafood place right on the water down there. Best grouper sandwich I ever had. They served it with these homemade kettle chips that were just perfectly crispy. The waiter was a young kid, maybe 19, told us he was studying marine biology at the local college. Smart kid, very polite. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, control. Yeah, no, I don't get anxious about that kind of stuff. It's literally just the high bridges. I don't know what it is about them, but nothing else really bothers me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever walked or biked across a high bridge, and if so, did you experience anxiety in that situation?\n\nPatient: Walk or bike? Oh, no, I haven't tried that. I haven't ridden a bike in... gosh, it's gotta be fifteen years. I used to have this great mountain bike, a Schwinn, back when we lived in our old neighborhood. It had twenty-one gears and these really thick tires. I used to ride it down to the corner store on Saturday mornings to get the paper and a couple of scratch-offs. Never won more than five bucks, though. My neighbor, Tom, he actually won like fifty grand on a scratch-off once. Can you believe that? He used it to put a sunroom on the back of his house. It was really nice, all glass, but it got so hot in the summer you couldn't even sit out there. He had to buy this massive ceiling fan just to keep the air moving. I helped him install it, actually. We almost dropped the motor right on his glass coffee table. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, walking or biking over a bridge. Yeah, no, I haven't done that. I can't even imagine it. If just driving over one or seeing it from the passenger seat gets me sweating and my chest all tight, I think walking across one would probably be a hundred times worse. Just being out there in the open air, looking down... no thanks. I wouldn't even try it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine if the anxiety is cued by the visual stimulus of the bridge", "question": "Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your anxiety, or is it something else about the experience of being on the bridge?"}

Well, it's... it's definitely just the sight of it. Like, as soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. I just end up gripping it so hard my knuckles turn white, and I have to stare straight ahead, like I just have to make it to the other side as fast as possible. It's the same feeling I got when I had to give that presentation at work last year. We were rolling out this new benefits package, and my boss made me stand up in front of the whole department. I hate public speaking. My mouth gets dry as a bone. I actually kept a bottle of water up there with me, one of those fancy Fiji waters in the square bottle. My wife buys them by the case at Costco. You know, Costco is great, but you end up buying way more than you need. We went in for paper towels once and came out with a gazebo. A whole gazebo! Took me three weekends to put it together in the backyard. The instructions were terrible, just pictures, no words. 

But, uh... where was I? Oh, the bridges. Yeah, it's just seeing them. I don't even have to be on it yet. Just knowing I have to cross it and seeing it there... that's what gets me going.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I keep planning my whole day around not having to drive over those high bridges. Like that big suspension one on the interstate? They built that back in, what, '89? My buddy Carl actually worked on the construction crew for it. He used to tell me these crazy stories about the scaffolding and how the wind would just howl up there. Carl's retired now, actually. Moved down to Florida, bought a pontoon boat... I don't know how he deals with a boat, honestly, the maintenance alone is a total nightmare. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, yeah. The bridges. If I absolutely can't find a route around them, I just white-knuckle the steering wheel the whole way across. Just gripping it for dear life. But mostly I take the long way around to avoid them, which makes me late for work sometimes. And working in HR, I really shouldn't be late. We had this one guy last week showing up at 10 AM every single day, just strolling in like he owned the place, and I had to be the one to sit him down and talk about company time... but yeah, the driving thing is just getting to be a real hassle.\n\nDoctor: Do you also avoid other high places, like tall buildings or mountainous areas, or is it mainly just the bridges that bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the bridges. Tall buildings don't bother me at all. Actually, our old HR office used to be on the 14th floor of this big high-rise downtown before we moved out to the business park. The elevators in that place, though... man, they were always breaking down. I swear, at least once a month somebody would get stuck in one. We had this maintenance guy, Hector—great guy, knew everything about HVAC—but he was always complaining about the elevator parts being on backorder. Supply chain issues, you know? \n\nIt's like you can't get anything on time anymore. I ordered a new filter for my home furnace like three weeks ago and it's still just sitting in some warehouse in Ohio, according to the tracking number. And you really need to change those every few months or your heating bill goes right through the roof. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, heights. Yeah, no, mountains and buildings are totally fine. Just those high bridges for some reason.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that the bridge will collapse while you are driving on it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think they're going to collapse. I mean, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every single day without a problem. My brother-in-law commutes over one every morning in his Chevy Silverado. Actually, he just got new tires on that thing, those big all-terrain ones. He got a flat last month on his way to work and had to wait three hours for a tow truck. Three hours! I told him he should have just put the spare on himself, but he said the jack was rusted out. You really gotta check your tools before winter hits, you know? The salt on the roads around here just eats right through the metal. I usually take my car to the wash at least twice a month during the winter just to spray the undercarriage. They have this new automated one down by the grocery store that does a really good job with the wax, too, and it's only like eight bucks if you buy the monthly pass. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the bridge falling down. Yeah, I'm not worried about the engineering or anything like that. It's just... when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. Like I'm just going to veer off the edge or something.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety occur when you are a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is it only when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I haven't actually been a passenger over one in a long time. My wife usually makes me drive everywhere. She hates driving, especially at night. Her night vision isn't what it used to be. We actually went to the eye doctor last week, Dr. Henderson over in the strip mall next to the Target? Nice guy, but his waiting room is freezing. I don't know why doctors' offices always keep the AC cranked up so high. You sit there for twenty minutes and you're shivering by the time they call your name. Anyway, he told her she needs bifocals now, which she wasn't too happy about. She thinks they make her look old, but I told her, hey, they have those progressive lenses now where you can't even see the line. My buddy Carl has them and he swears by them. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... what was the question? Oh, being a passenger. Yeah, I don't know, honestly. I'm almost always the one behind the wheel, so I couldn't really tell you if it's just as bad when I'm riding shotgun. I just know when I'm driving, it's... it's a whole ordeal.\n\nDoctor: If you were a passenger in a car going over a high bridge, would you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'd still be pretty worked up even if I wasn't the one driving. I mean, just the sight of those bridges gets to me. As soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating. Even if I was in the passenger seat, I think I'd still be gripping the door handle or something, just staring straight ahead, feeling like we just have to make it to the other side, you know? \n\nIt's funny, speaking of the passenger seat, my wife has this habit of leaving her empty coffee cups in the door pocket on her side. I swear, every time I go to vacuum out the car—which I try to do every other weekend, especially with the dog tracking in dirt—I find like three of those paper cups from the Dunkin' down the street. I keep telling her, \"Honey, just throw them out when we get gas,\" but she always forgets. We've got this great little hand vac, though, a Dirt Devil. Got it on sale at Home Depot last Black Friday. Best thirty bucks I ever spent. It gets right into the crevices of the seats. \n\nBut, uh, wait... oh, right, being a passenger on the bridge. Yeah, I'm pretty sure my chest would still get tight and I'd be sweating just as bad, even if I wasn't the one steering. Just seeing the bridge is enough to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding bridges or being late to work due to taking the long way around them affected your relationships with your coworkers or your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't really messed up my job or anything. I still get to work every day and do my job in HR, and I see people just fine. I just have to leave extra early to take the long way around the bridges. It's not a huge deal, but I do have to wake up at like 5:30 AM now. Which is fine, I guess, because I like to catch the morning news anyway. You know, they have that new anchor on Channel 4, the guy with the really great hair? He used to do the weather in Chicago, I think. My wife thinks he's too loud, but I like him. He actually did a whole segment on how to properly winterize your lawn sprinkler system last week. I meant to write down the steps, because last year one of my pipes burst and it was a nightmare. Water everywhere, right in the front yard. Had to call a plumber on a Sunday, which you know costs an arm and a leg. He charged me double just for showing up. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, work and stuff. Yeah, my job's fine. It's more just... I don't know, it makes me feel less independent than I used to be. Like, I've had to turn down a few fishing outings with the guys because the lake is over the big suspension bridge. I just told them I was busy, but really I just didn't want to deal with the drive. It's a shame, too, because they caught a ton of bass last time. My buddy Steve has this new fiberglass boat, a real beauty. He got it at a boat show in February. I think he paid too much for it, honestly, but he loves it. Anyway, yeah, I still get by, it's just... annoying having to plan around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety in other situations where you feel like you're not in control?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think it's about being in control, because like I said, even if I'm not driving, just seeing the bridge gets me all worked up. It's really just the bridges. I fly on planes just fine, you know? Actually, my wife and I flew down to Florida last winter to visit her sister in Tampa. We flew Southwest. You know, they let you check two bags for free, which is great because my wife always overpacks. She brought like four pairs of shoes for a four-day trip. We went to this amazing seafood place right on the water down there. Best grouper sandwich I ever had. They served it with these homemade kettle chips that were just perfectly crispy. The waiter was a young kid, maybe 19, told us he was studying marine biology at the local college. Smart kid, very polite. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, control. Yeah, no, I don't get anxious about that kind of stuff. It's literally just the high bridges. I don't know what it is about them, but nothing else really bothers me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever walked or biked across a high bridge, and if so, did you experience anxiety in that situation?\n\nPatient: Walk or bike? Oh, no, I haven't tried that. I haven't ridden a bike in... gosh, it's gotta be fifteen years. I used to have this great mountain bike, a Schwinn, back when we lived in our old neighborhood. It had twenty-one gears and these really thick tires. I used to ride it down to the corner store on Saturday mornings to get the paper and a couple of scratch-offs. Never won more than five bucks, though. My neighbor, Tom, he actually won like fifty grand on a scratch-off once. Can you believe that? He used it to put a sunroom on the back of his house. It was really nice, all glass, but it got so hot in the summer you couldn't even sit out there. He had to buy this massive ceiling fan just to keep the air moving. I helped him install it, actually. We almost dropped the motor right on his glass coffee table. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, walking or biking over a bridge. Yeah, no, I haven't done that. I can't even imagine it. If just driving over one or seeing it from the passenger seat gets me sweating and my chest all tight, I think walking across one would probably be a hundred times worse. Just being out there in the open air, looking down... no thanks. I wouldn't even try it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your anxiety, or is it something else about the experience of being on the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely just the sight of it. Like, as soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. I just end up gripping it so hard my knuckles turn white, and I have to stare straight ahead, like I just have to make it to the other side as fast as possible. It's the same feeling I got when I had to give that presentation at work last year. We were rolling out this new benefits package, and my boss made me stand up in front of the whole department. I hate public speaking. My mouth gets dry as a bone. I actually kept a bottle of water up there with me, one of those fancy Fiji waters in the square bottle. My wife buys them by the case at Costco. You know, Costco is great, but you end up buying way more than you need. We went in for paper towels once and came out with a gazebo. A whole gazebo! Took me three weekends to put it together in the backyard. The instructions were terrible, just pictures, no words. \n\nBut, uh... where was I? Oh, the bridges. Yeah, it's just seeing them. I don't even have to be on it yet. Just knowing I have to cross it and seeing it there... that's what gets me going.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety in other situations where you feel like you're not in control?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think it's about being in control, because like I said, even if I'm not driving, just seeing the bridge gets me all worked up. It's really just the bridges. I fly on planes just fine, you know? Actually, my wife and I flew down to Florida last winter to visit her sister in Tampa. We flew Southwest. You know, they let you check two bags for free, which is great because my wife always overpacks. She brought like four pairs of shoes for a four-day trip. We went to this amazing seafood place right on the water down there. Best grouper sandwich I ever had. They served it with these homemade kettle chips that were just perfectly crispy. The waiter was a young kid, maybe 19, told us he was studying marine biology at the local college. Smart kid, very polite. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, control. Yeah, no, I don't get anxious about that kind of stuff. It's literally just the high bridges. I don't know what it is about them, but nothing else really bothers me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever walked or biked across a high bridge, and if so, did you experience anxiety in that situation?\n\nPatient: Walk or bike? Oh, no, I haven't tried that. I haven't ridden a bike in... gosh, it's gotta be fifteen years. I used to have this great mountain bike, a Schwinn, back when we lived in our old neighborhood. It had twenty-one gears and these really thick tires. I used to ride it down to the corner store on Saturday mornings to get the paper and a couple of scratch-offs. Never won more than five bucks, though. My neighbor, Tom, he actually won like fifty grand on a scratch-off once. Can you believe that? He used it to put a sunroom on the back of his house. It was really nice, all glass, but it got so hot in the summer you couldn't even sit out there. He had to buy this massive ceiling fan just to keep the air moving. I helped him install it, actually. We almost dropped the motor right on his glass coffee table. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, walking or biking over a bridge. Yeah, no, I haven't done that. I can't even imagine it. If just driving over one or seeing it from the passenger seat gets me sweating and my chest all tight, I think walking across one would probably be a hundred times worse. Just being out there in the open air, looking down... no thanks. I wouldn't even try it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your anxiety, or is it something else about the experience of being on the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely just the sight of it. Like, as soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. I just end up gripping it so hard my knuckles turn white, and I have to stare straight ahead, like I just have to make it to the other side as fast as possible. It's the same feeling I got when I had to give that presentation at work last year. We were rolling out this new benefits package, and my boss made me stand up in front of the whole department. I hate public speaking. My mouth gets dry as a bone. I actually kept a bottle of water up there with me, one of those fancy Fiji waters in the square bottle. My wife buys them by the case at Costco. You know, Costco is great, but you end up buying way more than you need. We went in for paper towels once and came out with a gazebo. A whole gazebo! Took me three weekends to put it together in the backyard. The instructions were terrible, just pictures, no words. \n\nBut, uh... where was I? Oh, the bridges. Yeah, it's just seeing them. I don't even have to be on it yet. Just knowing I have to cross it and seeing it there... that's what gets me going.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your anxiety triggered by the height of the bridge or by the fact that you have to cross over water or a deep gap?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / human_resources_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every day, but when I get near one my mind acts like the car could go right over the side.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... I think it's the height, mostly. And the water. I know those bridges over the bay are built fine, you know? Like, folks drive them every day without a second thought. But when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. It's crazy. Speaking of cars, I actually just had to take my Honda in for an oil change last week. The guy at the shop, Jerry, he's been working on my cars for years. He tried to tell me I needed new brake pads, but I just had them done like, I don't know, maybe two years ago? I told him to just do the oil. They always try to upsell you on something, you know? Like the air filters. I can change an air filter myself in five minutes, I'm not paying them forty bucks to do it. Jerry's a good guy, though. He coaches Little League with my brother-in-law. They had a really good season last year, made it all the way to the regionals. My nephew plays first base. Kid's got a swing like you wouldn't believe, but he strikes out a lot because he always swings for the fences. 

But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the gap. Yeah, it's just... looking over the edge, seeing how far down it is to the water. It just makes me feel like we're gonna fall right off.

He used to tell me these crazy stories about the scaffolding and how the wind would just howl up there. Carl's retired now, actually. Moved down to Florida, bought a pontoon boat... I don't know how he deals with a boat, honestly, the maintenance alone is a total nightmare. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, yeah. The bridges. If I absolutely can't find a route around them, I just white-knuckle the steering wheel the whole way across. Just gripping it for dear life. But mostly I take the long way around to avoid them, which makes me late for work sometimes. And working in HR, I really shouldn't be late. We had this one guy last week showing up at 10 AM every single day, just strolling in like he owned the place, and I had to be the one to sit him down and talk about company time... but yeah, the driving thing is just getting to be a real hassle.\n\nDoctor: Do you also avoid other high places, like tall buildings or mountainous areas, or is it mainly just the bridges that bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the bridges. Tall buildings don't bother me at all. Actually, our old HR office used to be on the 14th floor of this big high-rise downtown before we moved out to the business park. The elevators in that place, though... man, they were always breaking down. I swear, at least once a month somebody would get stuck in one. We had this maintenance guy, Hector—great guy, knew everything about HVAC—but he was always complaining about the elevator parts being on backorder. Supply chain issues, you know? \n\nIt's like you can't get anything on time anymore. I ordered a new filter for my home furnace like three weeks ago and it's still just sitting in some warehouse in Ohio, according to the tracking number. Yeah, no, mountains and buildings are totally fine. I mean, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every single day without a problem. My brother-in-law commutes over one every morning in his Chevy Silverado. Actually, he just got new tires on that thing, those big all-terrain ones. He got a flat last month on his way to work and had to wait three hours for a tow truck. Three hours! I told him he should have just put the spare on himself, but he said the jack was rusted out. You really gotta check your tools before winter hits, you know? They have this new automated one down by the grocery store that does a really good job with the wax, too, and it's only like eight bucks if you buy the monthly pass. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the bridge falling down. Yeah, I'm not worried about the engineering or anything like that. It's just... when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. Like I'm just going to veer off the edge or something.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety occur when you are a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is it only when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I haven't actually been a passenger over one in a long time. My wife usually makes me drive everywhere. She hates driving, especially at night. Her night vision isn't what it used to be. We actually went to the eye doctor last week, Dr. Henderson over in the strip mall next to the Target? Nice guy, but his waiting room is freezing. I don't know why doctors' offices always keep the AC cranked up so high. You sit there for twenty minutes and you're shivering by the time they call your name. Anyway, he told her she needs bifocals now, which she wasn't too happy about. She thinks they make her look old, but I told her, hey, they have those progressive lenses now where you can't even see the line. My buddy Carl has them and he swears by them. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... what was the question? Oh, being a passenger. Yeah, I don't know, honestly. I'm almost always the one behind the wheel, so I couldn't really tell you if it's just as bad when I'm riding shotgun. I just know when I'm driving, it's... it's a whole ordeal.\n\nDoctor: If you were a passenger in a car going over a high bridge, would you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'd still be pretty worked up even if I wasn't the one driving. I mean, just the sight of those bridges gets to me. As soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating. Even if I was in the passenger seat, I think I'd still be gripping the door handle or something, just staring straight ahead, feeling like we just have to make it to the other side, you know? \n\nIt's funny, speaking of the passenger seat, my wife has this habit of leaving her empty coffee cups in the door pocket on her side. I swear, every time I go to vacuum out the car—which I try to do every other weekend, especially with the dog tracking in dirt—I find like three of those paper cups from the Dunkin' down the street. I keep telling her, \"Honey, just throw them out when we get gas,\" but she always forgets. We've got this great little hand vac, though, a Dirt Devil. Got it on sale at Home Depot last Black Friday. Best thirty bucks I ever spent. It gets right into the crevices of the seats. \n\nBut, uh, wait... oh, right, being a passenger on the bridge. Yeah, I'm pretty sure my chest would still get tight and I'd be sweating just as bad, even if I wasn't the one steering. Just seeing the bridge is enough to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding bridges or being late to work due to taking the long way around them affected your relationships with your coworkers or your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't really messed up my job or anything. You know, they have that new anchor on Channel 4, the guy with the really great hair? He used to do the weather in Chicago, I think. My wife thinks he's too loud, but I like him. I fly on planes just fine, you know? Actually, my wife and I flew down to Florida last winter to visit her sister in Tampa. We flew Southwest. You know, they let you check two bags for free, which is great because my wife always overpacks. She brought like four pairs of shoes for a four-day trip. We went to this amazing seafood place right on the water down there. Best grouper sandwich I ever had. They served it with these homemade kettle chips that were just perfectly crispy. The waiter was a young kid, maybe 19, told us he was studying marine biology at the local college. Smart kid, very polite. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, control. Yeah, no, I don't get anxious about that kind of stuff. It's literally just the high bridges. I don't know what it is about them, but nothing else really bothers me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever walked or biked across a high bridge, and if so, did you experience anxiety in that situation?\n\nPatient: Walk or bike? Oh, no, I haven't tried that. I haven't ridden a bike in... gosh, it's gotta be fifteen years. I used to have this great mountain bike, a Schwinn, back when we lived in our old neighborhood. It had twenty-one gears and these really thick tires. I used to ride it down to the corner store on Saturday mornings to get the paper and a couple of scratch-offs. Never won more than five bucks, though. My neighbor, Tom, he actually won like fifty grand on a scratch-off once. Can you believe that? He used it to put a sunroom on the back of his house. It was really nice, all glass, but it got so hot in the summer you couldn't even sit out there. He had to buy this massive ceiling fan just to keep the air moving. I helped him install it, actually. We almost dropped the motor right on his glass coffee table. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, walking or biking over a bridge. Yeah, no, I haven't done that. I can't even imagine it. If just driving over one or seeing it from the passenger seat gets me sweating and my chest all tight, I think walking across one would probably be a hundred times worse. Just being out there in the open air, looking down... no thanks. I wouldn't even try it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your anxiety, or is it something else about the experience of being on the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely just the sight of it. Like, as soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. I just end up gripping it so hard my knuckles turn white, and I have to stare straight ahead, like I just have to make it to the other side as fast as possible. It's the same feeling I got when I had to give that presentation at work last year. We were rolling out this new benefits package, and my boss made me stand up in front of the whole department. I hate public speaking. My mouth gets dry as a bone. I actually kept a bottle of water up there with me, one of those fancy Fiji waters in the square bottle. My wife buys them by the case at Costco. You know, Costco is great, but you end up buying way more than you need. We went in for paper towels once and came out with a gazebo. A whole gazebo! Took me three weekends to put it together in the backyard. The instructions were terrible, just pictures, no words. \n\nBut, uh... where was I? Oh, the bridges. Yeah, it's just seeing them. I don't even have to be on it yet. Just knowing I have to cross it and seeing it there... that's what gets me going.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety triggered by the height of the bridge or by the fact that you have to cross over water or a deep gap?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I think it's the height, mostly. And the water. I know those bridges over the bay are built fine, you know? Like, folks drive them every day without a second thought. But when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. It's crazy. Speaking of cars, I actually just had to take my Honda in for an oil change last week. The guy at the shop, Jerry, he's been working on my cars for years. He tried to tell me I needed new brake pads, but I just had them done like, I don't know, maybe two years ago? I told him to just do the oil. They always try to upsell you on something, you know? Like the air filters. I can change an air filter myself in five minutes, I'm not paying them forty bucks to do it. Jerry's a good guy, though. He coaches Little League with my brother-in-law. They had a really good season last year, made it all the way to the regionals. My nephew plays first base. Kid's got a swing like you wouldn't believe, but he strikes out a lot because he always swings for the fences. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the gap. Yeah, it's just... looking over the edge, seeing how far down it is to the water. It just makes me feel like we're gonna fall right off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, you know, I... I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. I don't think I cross a lot of high bridges over land. Most of the ones around here go over the water, like the big one heading out towards the shore. We used to go to the shore every summer when the kids were little. Rented a house down in Cape May. Beautiful Victorian houses down there. My wife loved the porches, always wanted to put a wrap-around porch on our house, but the zoning board wouldn't let us because of how close we are to the property line. The guy next door, Mr. Henderson, he's a real stickler for the rules. He actually called the town on us once because our grass was, like, half an inch over the limit. Can you believe that? Half an inch! I had to go out there on a Sunday morning and mow it just to get him to back off. My mower is getting pretty old, too. It's a Toro, had it for maybe twelve years. Still starts on the first pull, though, if you prime it right. You gotta push that little red bulb exactly three times, no more, no less. 

But, uh, anyway... over land or over water? I guess I just don't really know for sure since I try to avoid them all anyway.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I keep planning my whole day around not having to drive over those high bridges. Like that big suspension one on the interstate? They built that back in, what, '89? My buddy Carl actually worked on the construction crew for it. He used to tell me these crazy stories about the scaffolding and how the wind would just howl up there. Carl's retired now, actually. Moved down to Florida, bought a pontoon boat... I don't know how he deals with a boat, honestly, the maintenance alone is a total nightmare. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, yeah. The bridges. If I absolutely can't find a route around them, I just white-knuckle the steering wheel the whole way across. Just gripping it for dear life. But mostly I take the long way around to avoid them, which makes me late for work sometimes. And working in HR, I really shouldn't be late. We had this one guy last week showing up at 10 AM every single day, just strolling in like he owned the place, and I had to be the one to sit him down and talk about company time... but yeah, the driving thing is just getting to be a real hassle.\n\nDoctor: Do you also avoid other high places, like tall buildings or mountainous areas, or is it mainly just the bridges that bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the bridges. Tall buildings don't bother me at all. Actually, our old HR office used to be on the 14th floor of this big high-rise downtown before we moved out to the business park. The elevators in that place, though... man, they were always breaking down. I swear, at least once a month somebody would get stuck in one. We had this maintenance guy, Hector—great guy, knew everything about HVAC—but he was always complaining about the elevator parts being on backorder. Supply chain issues, you know? \n\nIt's like you can't get anything on time anymore. I ordered a new filter for my home furnace like three weeks ago and it's still just sitting in some warehouse in Ohio, according to the tracking number. And you really need to change those every few months or your heating bill goes right through the roof. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, heights. Yeah, no, mountains and buildings are totally fine. Just those high bridges for some reason.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid that the bridge will collapse while you are driving on it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think they're going to collapse. I mean, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every single day without a problem. My brother-in-law commutes over one every morning in his Chevy Silverado. Actually, he just got new tires on that thing, those big all-terrain ones. He got a flat last month on his way to work and had to wait three hours for a tow truck. Three hours! I told him he should have just put the spare on himself, but he said the jack was rusted out. You really gotta check your tools before winter hits, you know? The salt on the roads around here just eats right through the metal. I usually take my car to the wash at least twice a month during the winter just to spray the undercarriage. They have this new automated one down by the grocery store that does a really good job with the wax, too, and it's only like eight bucks if you buy the monthly pass. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the bridge falling down. Yeah, I'm not worried about the engineering or anything like that. It's just... when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. Like I'm just going to veer off the edge or something.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety occur when you are a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is it only when you are the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I haven't actually been a passenger over one in a long time. My wife usually makes me drive everywhere. She hates driving, especially at night. Her night vision isn't what it used to be. We actually went to the eye doctor last week, Dr. Henderson over in the strip mall next to the Target? Nice guy, but his waiting room is freezing. I don't know why doctors' offices always keep the AC cranked up so high. You sit there for twenty minutes and you're shivering by the time they call your name. Anyway, he told her she needs bifocals now, which she wasn't too happy about. She thinks they make her look old, but I told her, hey, they have those progressive lenses now where you can't even see the line. My buddy Carl has them and he swears by them. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... what was the question? Oh, being a passenger. Yeah, I don't know, honestly. I'm almost always the one behind the wheel, so I couldn't really tell you if it's just as bad when I'm riding shotgun. I just know when I'm driving, it's... it's a whole ordeal.\n\nDoctor: If you were a passenger in a car going over a high bridge, would you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'd still be pretty worked up even if I wasn't the one driving. I mean, just the sight of those bridges gets to me. As soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating. Even if I was in the passenger seat, I think I'd still be gripping the door handle or something, just staring straight ahead, feeling like we just have to make it to the other side, you know? \n\nIt's funny, speaking of the passenger seat, my wife has this habit of leaving her empty coffee cups in the door pocket on her side. I swear, every time I go to vacuum out the car—which I try to do every other weekend, especially with the dog tracking in dirt—I find like three of those paper cups from the Dunkin' down the street. I keep telling her, \"Honey, just throw them out when we get gas,\" but she always forgets. We've got this great little hand vac, though, a Dirt Devil. Got it on sale at Home Depot last Black Friday. Best thirty bucks I ever spent. It gets right into the crevices of the seats. \n\nBut, uh, wait... oh, right, being a passenger on the bridge. Yeah, I'm pretty sure my chest would still get tight and I'd be sweating just as bad, even if I wasn't the one steering. Just seeing the bridge is enough to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding bridges or being late to work due to taking the long way around them affected your relationships with your coworkers or your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't really messed up my job or anything. I still get to work every day and do my job in HR, and I see people just fine. I just have to leave extra early to take the long way around the bridges. It's not a huge deal, but I do have to wake up at like 5:30 AM now. Which is fine, I guess, because I like to catch the morning news anyway. You know, they have that new anchor on Channel 4, the guy with the really great hair? He used to do the weather in Chicago, I think. My wife thinks he's too loud, but I like him. He actually did a whole segment on how to properly winterize your lawn sprinkler system last week. I meant to write down the steps, because last year one of my pipes burst and it was a nightmare. Water everywhere, right in the front yard. Had to call a plumber on a Sunday, which you know costs an arm and a leg. He charged me double just for showing up. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, work and stuff. Yeah, my job's fine. It's more just... I don't know, it makes me feel less independent than I used to be. Like, I've had to turn down a few fishing outings with the guys because the lake is over the big suspension bridge. I just told them I was busy, but really I just didn't want to deal with the drive. It's a shame, too, because they caught a ton of bass last time. My buddy Steve has this new fiberglass boat, a real beauty. He got it at a boat show in February. I think he paid too much for it, honestly, but he loves it. Anyway, yeah, I still get by, it's just... annoying having to plan around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety in other situations where you feel like you're not in control?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think it's about being in control, because like I said, even if I'm not driving, just seeing the bridge gets me all worked up. It's really just the bridges. I fly on planes just fine, you know? Actually, my wife and I flew down to Florida last winter to visit her sister in Tampa. We flew Southwest. You know, they let you check two bags for free, which is great because my wife always overpacks. She brought like four pairs of shoes for a four-day trip. We went to this amazing seafood place right on the water down there. Best grouper sandwich I ever had. They served it with these homemade kettle chips that were just perfectly crispy. The waiter was a young kid, maybe 19, told us he was studying marine biology at the local college. Smart kid, very polite. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, control. Yeah, no, I don't get anxious about that kind of stuff. It's literally just the high bridges. I don't know what it is about them, but nothing else really bothers me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever walked or biked across a high bridge, and if so, did you experience anxiety in that situation?\n\nPatient: Walk or bike? Oh, no, I haven't tried that. I haven't ridden a bike in... gosh, it's gotta be fifteen years. I used to have this great mountain bike, a Schwinn, back when we lived in our old neighborhood. It had twenty-one gears and these really thick tires. I used to ride it down to the corner store on Saturday mornings to get the paper and a couple of scratch-offs. Never won more than five bucks, though. My neighbor, Tom, he actually won like fifty grand on a scratch-off once. Can you believe that? He used it to put a sunroom on the back of his house. It was really nice, all glass, but it got so hot in the summer you couldn't even sit out there. He had to buy this massive ceiling fan just to keep the air moving. I helped him install it, actually. We almost dropped the motor right on his glass coffee table. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, walking or biking over a bridge. Yeah, no, I haven't done that. I can't even imagine it. If just driving over one or seeing it from the passenger seat gets me sweating and my chest all tight, I think walking across one would probably be a hundred times worse. Just being out there in the open air, looking down... no thanks. I wouldn't even try it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your anxiety, or is it something else about the experience of being on the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely just the sight of it. Like, as soon as I see the bridge coming up in the distance, my chest gets all tight, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. I just end up gripping it so hard my knuckles turn white, and I have to stare straight ahead, like I just have to make it to the other side as fast as possible. It's the same feeling I got when I had to give that presentation at work last year. We were rolling out this new benefits package, and my boss made me stand up in front of the whole department. I hate public speaking. My mouth gets dry as a bone. I actually kept a bottle of water up there with me, one of those fancy Fiji waters in the square bottle. My wife buys them by the case at Costco. You know, Costco is great, but you end up buying way more than you need. We went in for paper towels once and came out with a gazebo. A whole gazebo! Took me three weekends to put it together in the backyard. The instructions were terrible, just pictures, no words. \n\nBut, uh... where was I? Oh, the bridges. Yeah, it's just seeing them. I don't even have to be on it yet. Just knowing I have to cross it and seeing it there... that's what gets me going.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety triggered by the height of the bridge or by the fact that you have to cross over water or a deep gap?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I think it's the height, mostly. And the water. I know those bridges over the bay are built fine, you know? Like, folks drive them every day without a second thought. But when I get near one, my mind just acts like the car could go right over the side. It's crazy. Speaking of cars, I actually just had to take my Honda in for an oil change last week. The guy at the shop, Jerry, he's been working on my cars for years. He tried to tell me I needed new brake pads, but I just had them done like, I don't know, maybe two years ago? I told him to just do the oil. They always try to upsell you on something, you know? Like the air filters. I can change an air filter myself in five minutes, I'm not paying them forty bucks to do it. Jerry's a good guy, though. He coaches Little League with my brother-in-law. They had a really good season last year, made it all the way to the regionals. My nephew plays first base. Kid's got a swing like you wouldn't believe, but he strikes out a lot because he always swings for the fences. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the gap. Yeah, it's just... looking over the edge, seeing how far down it is to the water. It just makes me feel like we're gonna fall right off.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety triggered more by the fact that the bridge is over water, or would you feel just as anxious crossing a high bridge over land?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, I... I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. I don't think I cross a lot of high bridges over land. Most of the ones around here go over the water, like the big one heading out towards the shore. We used to go to the shore every summer when the kids were little. Rented a house down in Cape May. Beautiful Victorian houses down there. My wife loved the porches, always wanted to put a wrap-around porch on our house, but the zoning board wouldn't let us because of how close we are to the property line.
